## MLLM - 100 in Total

| **[PhysCount: Do MLLMs Count Visual Appearances or Physical Entities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40530v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Images reveal appearances, but counting often asks about physical objects. A single entity may be visible directly, in several reflections, or through glass, so detecting every visible occurrence does not determine how many entities are present. Existing visual counting benchmarks rarely separate these two operations. We introduce PhysCount, a benchmark for evaluating whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can distinguish what appears in an image from what physically exists in the scene. PhysCount contains 546 real-world images and 4,595 human-written questions organized into 651 target-specific groups. For each group, annotators locate the relevant appearances, label how each is observed, and identify appearances of the same entity. Four complementary question families test the enumeration of appearances, the enumeration of physical entities, image-formation-specific counting, and appearance--entity correspondence. We measure both accuracy on each family and the proportion of groups answered entirely correctly. Across 40 MLLMs, counting visible appearances is substantially easier than recovering the identities that connect them. Even the strongest model answers every question correctly in only 13.36\% of groups. Performance declines when one entity produces many appearances or when its appearances cross observation modes. Higher image resolution improves access to visual evidence but brings limited gains in complete-group accuracy, whereas answering related questions jointly produces a marked improvement. These results expose a gap between recognizing what is visible and correctly recovering the physical entities behind those observations.</p></details> |  |
| **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. Existing video QA benchmarks leave this ability unmeasured: their questions are mutually independent, never reference one another, and each is answerable from the video alone, so nothing ever requires carrying information across turns. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Among over 20 evaluated open- and closed-source MLLMs, Claude Opus 5 performs the best with an overall score of only 45.7\%, highlighting the challenge of in-context video memory. Our dataset and evaluation are released.</p></details> |  |
| **[MLLM-GS: Multimodal Large Language Model Prior-Guided Fast Novel View Synthesis in Extreme Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39936v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Novel view synthesis (NVS) in degraded scenes aims to reconstruct 3D scenes from multi-view observations affected by various degradations, and to generate clear and photorealistic images at unseen viewpoints. Despite recent advances, existing methods often rely on degradation-specific models, limiting their generalization across diverse extreme environments. Generative models provide rich visual priors, but their outputs often suffer from pixel misalignment and color deviations, which can propagate into the reconstructed 3D scene and compromise reconstruction quality. Furthermore, the high computational and memory costs of 3D reconstruction hinder the practical application of NVS in extreme environments. To this end, we propose MLLM-GS, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) prior-guided framework for fast NVS in extreme environments. MLLM-GS consists of two branches: MLLM prior-guided view enhancement and fast 3D scene reconstruction. The former leverages the rich visual priors of a frozen MLLM to enhance views under different severe degradations and introduces a lightweight correction network to reduce pixel shifts and color distortions in MLLM outputs. With supervision from only a few paired clean views, this branch produces reliable enhanced views for all degraded observations, providing pseudo-GT views for subsequent 3D reconstruction. The latter improves 3DGS efficiency by optimizing rasterization, Gaussian memory layout, and parameter updates, reducing redundant computation, memory-access overhead, and optimization costs. Extensive experiments on multiple degraded scenes demonstrate that MLLM-GS effectively handles various severe degradations and achieves high-quality NVS with significantly lower reconstruction time and memory consumption. Project Page: https://wzlwlm13.github.io/MLLM-GS</p></details> |  |
| **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Accepted Paper</p></details> |
| **[Raincheck: Deferred Multimodal Prefill for Efficient Multi-Turn MLLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39562v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn conversations with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reuse input context, but long multimodal inputs increase time to first token (TTFT). Query-aware hard pruning reduces prefill tokens but permanently excludes information that later questions may require. We propose Raincheck, a framework for deferred multimodal prefill that decouples initial token selection from later information availability. An existing pruning method selects tokens for initial prefill; Raincheck preserves and progressively prefills unselected tokens in source order using unused compute capacity during decode. Unfinished prefill continues after answer return during available scheduling gaps. Attention visibility control and affine state mapping enable context updates after generation begins in full-attention and hybrid recurrent models. Prefill-decode fusion reduces repeated weight reads. We evaluate Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B with 30% initial multimodal-token retention. Across both models and four pruning methods, Raincheck recovers on average 77.7% and 60.4% of the quality lost to matched hard pruning on MultiVerse and ConvBench, respectively. On MultiVerse, the SGLang implementation reduces mean first-turn TTFT by up to 24.1% relative to full prefill, with less than 0.5% increase in mean time per output token (TPOT).</p></details> |  |
| **[What do MLLMs know about 'Waldo'? Evaluating Visual Reasoning under Multi-Instance Multi-Object Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38957v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to significant progress in visual reasoning capabilities. However, existing benchmarks curate image-question pairs pertaining to object categories with few instances, enabling trivial object referencing and limited visual ambiguity. In this work, we introduce MIMO-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in a Multi-Instance per Multi-Object categories (MIMO) setting. Each image-question pair in our benchmark pertains to instances belonging to object categories with multiple instances. The presence of such similar feature distractors causes hindrance in basic visual reasoning tasks, necessitating the referring expression to be composed of multiple visual subtasks to uniquely and unambiguously identify the target object. MIMO-Bench comprises 3,802 image-question pairs across attribute binding and numerical counting tasks. Despite impressive performances on existing benchmarks, both SOTA open-source (InternVL, QwenVL) and proprietary (Gemini, GPT-5) models exhibit substantially lower performance on MIMO-Bench, highlighting their limitations in the MIMO setting. The benchmark will be made publicly available.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where Can We Fool the Model's Mind? Response-Guided Semantic Probing and Adaptive Routing for Transferable Attacks on Black-Box MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38013v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) ground language in visual evidence, yet their semantic reasoning can be exploited through transferable black-box adversarial attacks. Existing attacks typically allocate perturbations using surrogate-model saliency, which may not reflect how an unseen MLLM semantically interprets or relies on different image regions. We introduce VISTA (Vulnerability-Informed Semantic Transfer Attacks), a black-box framework that identifies semantically vulnerable visual regions from an MLLM's responses without access to gradients, logits, parameters, or internal representations. VISTA supports two attack settings: (i) VISTA-Proxy, a pure-transfer setting that probes an open-source MLLM and transfers the resulting adversarial examples to unseen commercial MLLMs without querying them; and (ii) VISTA-Victim, a query-assisted setting that probes the target MLLM to capture model-specific semantic vulnerabilities. VISTA builds an Uncertainty-Based Multimodal Behavior (UBMB) map from response drift, conflict, misleading, and semantic shifts, then uses entropy-adaptive Sinkhorn routing and a surrogate ensemble to focus perturbations on vulnerable regions. Across GPT-5, Gemini-3.5-Flash, and Claude Sonnet 4.6, VISTA achieves up to 97 % attack success rate (ASR) while maintaining a Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) above 30 dB. Notably, VISTA-Proxy achieves up to 93 % ASR without querying the final victim, demonstrating that response-derived semantic vulnerability can enable effective transfer across heterogeneous MLLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerceptRL: Making Visual Perception Rewardable in MLLMs from Answers to Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37471v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained visual perception remains a bottleneck during post-training of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), as perceptual errors can constrain gains from reasoning-focused optimization. Although image-caption pre-training improves perception, its influence can weaken as subsequent stages change data mixtures and objectives. Continuing caption supervision during post-training is challenging because subjective, information-dense descriptions complicate verifiable reward design. We present PerceptRL, which strengthens perception at two complementary levels. Perception RL uses a multi-agent pipeline to extract verified errors from dense image captions and convert the corresponding visual facts into QA pairs with fixed answer labels and rule-based rewards. Chain QA links correlated errors, while multiple-choice QA combines unrelated errors. A specialized route targets high-error domains with tailored verification, and a domain-agnostic general route covers errors beyond these domains. Perception Agentic RL addresses fine-grained and dense cases unresolved by single-turn Perception RL through multi-turn inspection with visual tools. We observe coordinate drift even in models trained to use tools, where incorrect crops or annotations distort evidence and propagate errors. Coordinate Injection corrects spatial operations in cold-start trajectories, while a grounding-aware process reward supervises intermediate spatial fidelity alongside final-answer correctness. Training proceeds through shared cold-start SFT, single-turn Perception RL, and multi-turn Perception Agentic RL. Across seven benchmarks under a shared tool-use protocol, PerceptRL achieves 71.6% average accuracy, surpassing the base model with the same tools and the strongest evaluated frontier VLM by 15.7 and 5.2 percentage points, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[FullFront: Benchmarking MLLMs Across the Full Front-End Engineering Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17399v4)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Front-end engineering involves a complex workflow where engineers conceptualize designs, translate them into code, and iteratively refine the implementation. While recent benchmarks primarily focus on converting visual designs to code, we present FullFront, a benchmark designed to evaluate Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) \textbf{across the full front-end development pipeline}. FullFront assesses three fundamental tasks that map directly to the front-end engineering pipeline: Webpage Design (conceptualization phase), Webpage Perception QA (comprehension of visual organization and elements), and Webpage Code Generation (implementation phase). Unlike existing benchmarks that use either scraped websites with bloated code or oversimplified LLM-generated HTML, FullFront employs a novel, two-stage process to transform real-world webpages into clean, standardized HTML while maintaining diverse visual designs and avoiding copyright issues. Extensive testing of state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals significant limitations in page perception, code generation (particularly for image handling and layout), and interaction implementation. Our results quantitatively demonstrate performance disparities across models and tasks, and highlight a substantial gap between current MLLM capabilities and human expert performance in front-end engineering. The FullFront benchmark and code are available in https://github.com/Mikivishy/FullFront.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decoupling Consistency and Conflict across MLLM Layers for Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36569v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The trustworthiness of Web content faces a growing threat from multimodal fake news. Real news usually shows aligned image-text semantics, while fake news often contains a few conflicts at varying levels, such as mismatches in objects, scenes, events, and claims. However, existing methods usually treat consistency and conflict in a coarse and static way, limiting their ability to capture sparse conflicts and adapt to varying conflict levels. Inspired by the distinct knowledge encoded across layers in large language models, we propose Multi-Level Consistency and Conflict Decoupling (MLCCD), which captures conflicts at different levels from different layers of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Specifically, we first use the MLLM's final decision representation to select visual and textual tokens relevant to the task, preventing irrelevant content from diluting sparse conflicts. Then, we preserve tokens whose representations change sharply between adjacent layers, capturing the knowledge each layer adds. Finally, we decouple this knowledge into consistency and conflict evidence and aggregate each across layers with its own router, adapting to varying conflict levels. Experiments on three benchmarks show that MLCCD outperforms existing methods.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[Diagnosing and Correcting Bias in MLLM for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36210v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-video question answering is challenging since the answer often hinges on a few decisive moments scattered throughout the video, while memory constraints force Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to sample frames at extremely low rates, resulting in sparsity that risks omitting key evidence. Though aggressively enlarging the context size is intuitive, we argue that performance remains fundamentally constrained by the bias, \emph{i.e.}, the misleading cues caused by spurious \textit{linguistic} correlations and salient yet irrelevant \textit{visual} observations. In this paper, we first introduce {S-MME}, a benchmark designed to systematically diagnose shortcut-biased behavior in MLLMs. To mitigate this failure, we further propose a training-free framework, Counterfactual Long-video Evidence-Aware Reasoning (CLEAR). By treating the full video-question pair as a complete view, CLEAR estimates bias effects via augmented views by only keeping the question or locally salient visual clips. Then, we intervene hidden states to mitigate the discrepancy at inference such that the correct answer can be predicted confidently. With experimental studies across five benchmarks, we show that our method is generalizable and can be integrated in different MLLMs, yielding consistent gains in prediction and robustness. We hope CLEAR can offer a complementary direction in test-time scaling for long-video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 6 figures. Code:https://github.com/Xiaoqiovo/CLEAR</p></details> |
| **[MulFCoder: Framework-conditioned Multi-agent for MLLM-based Multi-framework Front-end Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34418v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating runnable front-end code from UI screenshots is a long-standing goal in automated software engineering. Existing MLLM-based methods predominantly focused on HTML/CSS, leaving multi-framework generation for React/Vue/Angular underexplored. Naively modifying prompts leads to substantial performance gaps across multi-framework and highly framework-specific error modes. To address this, we propose MulFCoder, a framework-conditioned multi-agent method that explicitly encodes framework constraints to bring multi-framework differences into a decidable rule space. MulFCoder orchestrates four agents: Grounder constructs an ElementTable, ContentTable, and macro-layout regions from detected UI elements; Planner builds a DOM-like hierarchical layout tree, produces a task schedule, and derives a framework-specific file Contract; Writer generates structured file writes or patches within a restricted edit window; Judger enforces lightweight, framework-conditioned constraints to accept or reject updates and trigger bounded repairs, preventing drift and deadlocks without expensive builds. Experiments demonstrate that MulFCoder substantially improves compilation success rate and reduces framework-specific errors, with particularly pronounced gains on Angular.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ICML 2026. 17 pages</p></details> |
| **[Do MLLMs Really See It: Reinforcing Visual Attention in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08241v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, existing approaches largely rely on long textual reasoning trajectories and provide limited mechanisms for learning stable visual attention policies. Our analysis shows that current MLLMs exhibit weak visual focus: early-stage visual misalignment is rarely corrected during subsequent reasoning, leading to error propagation and failed inferences. We argue that this limitation stems from inadequate credit assignment for visual attention during training. To address this issue, we propose SAYO, a visual reasoning model trained with a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that introduces a region-level visual attention-based reward. This reward explicitly aligns optimization signals with visually grounded reasoning steps, enabling the model to learn more reliable attention behaviors. Extensive experiments across multiple multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that SAYO consistently improves performance on diverse reasoning and perception tasks. Project is open at https://github.com/Cratileo/Sayo.</p></details> |  |
| **[Humans in Space: Can MLLMs Construct Human-Centric Spatial Understanding via Active Exocentric Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34149v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at zero-shot reasoning, their spatial capabilities are primarily evaluated through static, passive observation. In complex, human-centric scenes, occlusion often hides crucial geometric evidence, and passive benchmarks force models to guess rather than recognize their own visual uncertainty. To bridge this gap, we introduce SATE-Bench, a novel interactive benchmark comprising 316 real-world scenes and 2,525 multi-turn QA pairs to evaluate visual self-awareness and active exocentric view-exploration. It combines a Passive multi-view control with an Active exploration setting, where models start from a severely occluded view and must iteratively query adjacent viewpoints until they gather enough evidence to fully ``sate'' the question, scored by a step-penalized efficiency reward. Evaluating state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals that they terminate exploration prematurely while reporting high confidence, and struggle with fine-grained 3D geometric tasks; matched-view controls further attribute the Active--Passive gap primarily to insufficient evidence acquisition. Moreover, fine-tuning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) shows that rewarding view-seeking increases active exploration but lowers overall accuracy, as models over-explore and fail to fuse expanding multi-view contexts across turns. These findings indicate that scaling passive multi-view perception does not intrinsically yield active spatial reasoning, highlighting the need for models that know where to look, how to integrate what they see, and when to stop. Code and data are available at https://gtc-gh.github.io/SATE-Bench/</p></details> | <details><summary>Winte...</summary><p>Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision (WACV) 2027. 33 pages, 14 figures, and 23 tables</p></details> |
| **[InteractiveWorld: A Verifiable Human-Centric Environment for Building Better Interactive MLLM Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33966v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>People have long dreamed of an assistant that is always there to guide them and remind what they forget, from new dishes to lost keys. Powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs), egocentric AI assistants on wearable devices are bringing this dream within reach: they see what the user sees and give instructions for user's physical work when necessary. However, testing such assistance is challenging and usually requires real people. The expensive and rare real interaction data leaves a key question unanswered: Can today's MLLMs truly help people in physical world? To answer this question, we propose InteractiveWorld, the first verifiable environment for closed-loop interaction between human and AI assistant. Unlike existing robot-centric simulators, InteractiveWorld lets the assistant guide a human agent who observes the scene, understands instructions, and acts independently. It thus realistically simulates how AI assistant collaborates with human: the assistant instructs, the human acts, and the environment executes actions and updates what they see next. Building on it, we introduce InteractiveBench, the first dynamic benchmark for egocentric AI assistants, with 120 multi-turn tasks across six categories. In each task, the assistant guides the human in real time, and every instruction shapes the outcome, which is scored by verifiable, state-based rewards. Experiments with 12 frontier MLLMs show they are still far from reliable assistants. Even the strongest model, Claude Fable 5.1, achieves at most 20% and 25% on long-horizon Guidance and Planning tasks, respectively. Overall, our work offers a verifiable platform and a rigorous evaluation set, laying a solid foundation for research on interactive MLLM assistants that truly help people in the physical world.</p></details> |  |
| **[PathKGen: Pathology Knowledge Assisted MLLM for Explainable Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33691v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Whole Slide Image (WSI) analysis serves as the fundamental method for the clinical diagnosis of cancer in digital histopathology. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have enabled state-of-the-art (SOTA) automated histopathology report generation. However, MLLMs reason purely through distributions encoded in their neural parameters, limiting the amount of knowledge and reasoning logic they can contain given the model scale. Their black-box reasoning poses another challenge to the human interpretation of intermediate reasoning. We address these issues by utilizing knowledge graphs to inject external structured knowledge for MLLMs to reason over, while making this reasoning component visible. Knowledge graphs are capable of partially externalizing the containment of medical knowledge, an area that remains underexplored in pathology report generation. We introduce PathKGen, a knowledge graph-assisted MLLM for histopathology report generation. PathKGen has two main branches: (1) a visual path that learns visual token inputs to the MLLM using pathology foundation models, and (2) a knowledge path that learns knowledge token inputs by extracting key morphological features of WSIs through querying a pathology-specific knowledge graph. A comprehensive evaluation on WSI-Bench dataset shows that PathKGen surpasses previous SOTA methods while revealing diagnostic reasoning as visually intuitive graphs. Due to its modular design, the proposed knowledge path is complementary to vision-aligned MLLMs and can be extended to other applications by curating domain-specific knowledge graphs to generate knowledge tokens.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the WACV 2027. Source code is available at https://github.com/nadunnr/PathKGen</p></details> |
| **[Rescuing Your Pruning: Rethinking Visual Token Pruning and Post-Training Recovery in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33688v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning substantially reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), yet existing methods are mostly training-free and plug-and-play. They treat the resulting performance drop as an inevitable cost of compression and pursue increasingly complex pruning rules. We rethink this view through two questions: how much of the loss can post-training rescue, and, building on this recovery, how can joint compression push pruning to its limit? We curate a 282K-sample post-training mixture covering general VQA, reasoning, OCR, and grounding, and post-train 36 configurations that drop or merge tokens under a unified protocol. Post-training rescues most of the loss, even for OCR and grounding, and its adaptation gain in closing the gap between the pruned and the dense model grows as the budget tightens. For joint compression, we find that the vision encoder, not the LLM, dominates prefill time in native-resolution MLLMs, so where tokens are removed matters more than how many: keeping 40% of tokens at the encoder runs faster than keeping 10% at the projector. Our best configuration prunes the encoder lightly and the projector aggressively, leaves the LLM intact, and keeps general understanding and reasoning at the dense level. It accelerates the LLM stage by 3.49x but the end-to-end time-to-first-token by only 1.54x, showing that LLM-stage speedups overstate end-to-end gains. We hope these findings offer insights for future research on joint visual token compression. Our data, protocol, and code will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[TerraVis: Towards Evaluation of World-Grounded Visual Consistency in Text-to-Image Generation via MLLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02959v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent text-to-image models have made substantial progress in photorealism, aesthetics, and text-image alignment. Yet visually appealing images can still violate real-world plausibility, exhibiting malformed object structures, impossible anatomy, physically implausible interactions, or inconsistent spatial relationships. Such failures are not well captured by existing fidelity, aesthetics, preference, or alignment metrics. To address this gap, we introduce TerraVis, a framework for evaluating world-grounded visual consistency in generated images. TerraVis defines a structured taxonomy of world-consistency violations spanning object-, interaction-, and scene-level failures, and employs a multi-stage evaluation framework to identify and quantify them. Given an image, TerraVis first uses an MLLM to assess its eligibility for evaluation, then detects violations across 18 taxonomy-defined types and classifies them as minor or major to derive an overall world-consistency score. Across diverse open-source and proprietary text-to-image models on two widely used benchmarks, TerraVis achieves the strongest correlation with human judgments of world consistency among existing metrics. Our benchmark results further show that models that achieve strong performance on conventional metrics can still exhibit substantial world-consistency failures. These findings highlight world consistency as a complementary evaluation dimension and demonstrate that TerraVis enables systematic quantification, diagnosis, and comparison of such failures. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/ShyFoo/TerraVis.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (ED Track). Minor revisions</p></details> |
| **[The Answer Knows Where to Look: Zooming before MLLMs Answer under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) read images through visual tokens, and under the small per-image budgets that efficient deployment requires, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible. Zooming in recovers the evidence, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. We find that MLLMs know where the evidence is, but not in a form that can be read before they answer: the answer tokens localize it from the middle layers on, whereas the attention available before answering does so only in the top layers, at a depth that varies across models, and diffusely. Supervising the pre-answer attention to point degrades reading, and the benefit of zooming is governed by each image's information gap. Building on these findings, we introduce ours, which lets an MLLM look where its answer would. A dedicated look token, processed in a single prefill and kept out of the answering pass, learns in hindsight to attend as the model's own correct answers do, extracting this knowledge into a concentrated pointer before any answer exists. Its hidden state then selects how to look, from no zoom to crops, square windows, windows that adapt to the pointer and foveated warps, supervised by the counterfactual outcome of every view. ours needs no box annotations, per-model layer search, external localizers or generation before the answer. On Qwen3-VL at three scales and five text-rich benchmarks, it surpasses the strongest baseline by 4.8-9.1 points at 98 visual tokens per view and attention-guided zooming by 11.1-17.0 points, while running faster than attention-guided and grounding-based zooming.</p></details> |  |
| **[StemBind: When MLLMs Get Lost Between Rules and Instances in Abstract Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00148v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often know the rule but pick the wrong answer: on abstract visual reasoning (AVR) tasks, a model can describe what it sees and name the underlying pattern, yet still fail to choose the matching candidate. Existing AVR benchmarks cannot detect this because they collapse perception, rule induction, and answer selection into a single right-or-wrong signal. We introduce StemBind, a shared-stem diagnostic benchmark that probes the same visual stem with three aligned questions: Perception (what is in the image), Rule (what pattern governs it), and Full (which option completes it), so a final-answer error can be attributed to a specific sub-step on the same evidence. StemBind contains 2,298 curated knowledge-light stems across nine auditable visual operations, totaling 19,533 P/R/F tasks, with each full item annotated by Sternberg's four reasoning stages (S1 Encode, S2 Infer, S3 Map, S4 Apply). Evaluating 24 frontier MLLM configurations yields four findings. (i) The R-F chasm: rule accuracy exceeds full-item accuracy on 22 of 24 models, so most failures happen after the rule is identified. (ii) A persistent binding gap: even when P and R are both correct on the same stem, models still answer F incorrectly 51.2% of the time. (iii) The bottleneck is S3: process diagnostics and Stage-wise Stimulus Augmentation localize the dominant failure to rule-to-instance mapping. (iv) Scaling and thinking do not help: neither larger models nor explicit thinking mode reliably closes the gap, and thinking even lowers rule and full-item accuracy. StemBind reframes AVR evaluation from final-answer ranking to locating where abstract visual reasoning breaks down, identifying rule-to-instance binding as a concrete next target for vision-grounded reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). Camera-ready version. Project page: https://hexixiang.github.io/StemBind ; Code: https://github.com/hexixiang/StemBind ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/user48271/Stembind</p></details> |
| **[Making MLLM Latents Matter: Factor-Aware Predictive Learning for Cinematic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30926v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cinematic video generation requires both temporal inference from limited inputs (i.e., sparse rather than frame-by-frame guidance) and factor-aware control of character, scene, action, and camera. First, latent-only generator grounding trains the DiT with a frozen MLLM and connector, strengthening the DiT's use of MLLM latents for generation. Second, for factor-aware control, paired videos specify a target-factor change and non-target preservation, and factor-span replacement trains the DiT to realize this relationship in its output. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. Extensive experiments demonstrate our consistently better performance on three cinematic video generation tasks (i.e., sparse-to-video, factor-aware editing, and multi-shot generation) over baselines and validate FAPL's contributions to MLLM feature use, temporal control, and factor coordination.</p></details> |  |

Questions are constructed around visually observable events or event sequences, requiring models to notice, count, describe, or reason about transient evidence. We evaluate 34 proprietary and open-source MLLMs. Under the main evaluation settings, the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves 39.6% overall accuracy, while most open-source models remain below 25%. Diagnostic analyses show that denser frame sampling improves some models, but substantial errors persist even when sampled frames cover all annotated necessary event intervals in an audited subset. Performance also declines on longer videos, suggesting additional temporal-localization challenges. By exposing these limitations, Moment-Video provides a diagnostic benchmark to guide the evaluation and development of video MLLMs that more reliably understand brief, answer-critical visual events.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Static Cues: Unified Dynamic Detection of MLLM Backdoors via Dual-Flow Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20319v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical domains, yet their susceptibility to backdoor attacks remains a formidable challenge. While existing defenses have evolved from visual-centric patch cues to unsupervised internal attention statistics, they are fundamentally constrained by a reliance on static snapshots and attack-specific heuristics. Given the intricate interplay between dual modalities, the vast design space for multimodal triggers allows diverse threats, such as textual injections, sub-perceptual noises, and coordinated cross-modal triggers, to easily circumvent such rigid priors, particularly when attack types are unknown in real-world applications. In this paper, we rethink backdoor detection by shifting the diagnostic paradigm from static observations to dynamic output evolution. We observe that a successful attack inevitably hijacks the model's decoding trajectory, skewing internal modal contributions during generation while maintaining an abnormal output invariance despite input perturbations. To capture this behavior, we propose EVO-Detect, a unified detection framework that monitors the dynamic evolution of internal signals during early decoding steps through two complementary channels: Attention Flow and Logits Flow. The attention branch measures how image-text attention allocation evolves during early generation, whereas the logits branch quantifies the sensitivity of the output distribution to modal perturbations through interventional ablations. We then convert these trajectories into compact sample-level metrics and normalize them against negative-control signatures so that natural perturbations are less likely to be mistaken for backdoor evidence. Extensive evaluations show that our approach substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines such as BYE and TCAP across diverse attack families.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM MM 2026 (Oral). Code: https://github.com/kaoru0822-kitauji/EVO_Detect</p></details> |
| **[VisualWorldBench: A Fine-Grained Multi-Task Benchmark for Evaluating Visual World Knowledge in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20083v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual world knowledge is a core capability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), underpinning knowledge-intensive visual question answering and fine-grained visual reasoning. | **[EmoGenie: Benchmarking and Evaluating Expressed and Evoked Emotion in T2I Generation with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18455v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rapid advances in artificial intelligence-generated content (AIGC) have significantly promoted using text-to-image (T2I) models to generate images with specific affective intentions. | **[What Makes a Good Visual Representation for Spatial Understanding in MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial understanding is a necessary step in moving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) toward reasoning about the 3D physical world. What we transfer comes from 3D geometry models, self-supervised image and video models, vision-language models, and generative models, in the form of latent features or explicit depth and point maps. We find that a representation's 3D structure correlates strongly with how much it helps, measured by cross-view consistency for latent features and by depth and reconstruction accuracy for explicit geometry. We further show that the form of a representation dictates the mechanism it needs. Latent features gain as much from fusion as from distillation; explicit geometry gains little from fusion, and most of its distillation gain depends on a geometry decoding head. | **[Can MLLMs Reason About Visual Persuasion? | **[Confidence under Visual Token Pruning: Removed Evidence and Risk-Controlled Token Budgets for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12035v4)** | 2026-10-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning speeds up multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by keeping a small subset of the visual tokens, and pruning methods are compared by the accuracy they retain. Returning a few object tokens to the kept set recovers most of the lost accuracy. Selectors that avoid keeping redundant tokens stay close to the calibration of the unpruned model. We then use the confidence of the pruned model to set a per-question token budget. The model answers with few tokens first and again with all tokens when its confidence is low. Conformal risk control sets the threshold to bound the expected deviation from the unpruned model. | **[Decoding Affective Nuances: Enhancing MLLMs via Hierarchical Emotion Reasoning and Contrastive Discriminative Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36782v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated exceptional capabilities in objective understanding tasks, their performance in affective reasoning still falls significantly short of human standards. | **[Fine-tuning Multi-modal LLMs with ART: Art-based Reinforcement Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11854v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to specific tasks usually involves fine-tuning: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) edits the weights, while soft prompting prepends trainable virtual tokens. Both change how an already-optimized model is served, so serving many task-specific adapters at high throughput requires adapter-aware infrastructure. We propose Art-Based Reinforcement Training (ART), which optimizes a single auxiliary input image to specialize a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for a specific task. At inference time, ART simply includes this image in each standard multimodal prompt, requiring no changes to the serving engine. We compare ART with LoRA and soft prompting across five reasoning benchmarks for four small MLLMs from three architectures. While different methods perform best for different tasks, ART ranks first or second in 10 of 20 benchmark-MLLM pairs, with its best results on the two math word problem benchmarks (GSM8k and SVAMP). It is significantly ahead of the next best adapter in 2 cases, within the noise of the next best adapter in 6, and significantly above the text-only baseline in 18. ART therefore reaches accuracy close to LoRA and soft prompts while serving faster than LoRA. In the vLLM serving stack, ART reduces throughput by 6% to 14% relative to text-only requests, compared to LoRA's 13% to 93% slowdown. Beyond efficient serving, ART artifacts are plain images, making them easy to store, distribute, and share. This approach keep task-specific adaptation outside the model weights, so the same frozen model can support different tasks without separate model variants.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhysCount: Do MLLMs Count Visual Appearances or Physical Entities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40530v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Images reveal appearances, but counting often asks about physical objects. A single entity may be visible directly, in several reflections, or through glass, so detecting every visible occurrence does not determine how many entities are present. Existing visual counting benchmarks rarely separate these two operations. We introduce PhysCount, a benchmark for evaluating whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can distinguish what appears in an image from what physically exists in the scene. PhysCount contains 546 real-world images and 4,595 human-written questions organized into 651 target-specific groups. For each group, annotators locate the relevant appearances, label how each is observed, and identify appearances of the same entity. Four complementary question families test the enumeration of appearances, the enumeration of physical entities, image-formation-specific counting, and appearance--entity correspondence. We measure both accuracy on each family and the proportion of groups answered entirely correctly. Across 40 MLLMs, counting visible appearances is substantially easier than recovering the identities that connect them. Even the strongest model answers every question correctly in only 13.36\% of groups. Performance declines when one entity produces many appearances or when its appearances cross observation modes. Higher image resolution improves access to visual evidence but brings limited gains in complete-group accuracy, whereas answering related questions jointly produces a marked improvement. These results expose a gap between recognizing what is visible and correctly recovering the physical entities behind those observations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rocks, Pebbles and Sand: Modality-aware Scheduling for Multimodal Large Language Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26498v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) power platforms like ChatGPT, Gemini, and Copilot, enabling rich interactions across text, images, and videos. Unlike text-only workloads, multimodal requests are inherently heterogeneous, differing by orders of magnitude in latency and memory footprint. Existing LLM serving systems, designed for homogeneous text workloads, struggle under this heterogeneity: large image and video requests monopolize GPU resources, causing severe head-of-line blocking and degrading the responsiveness of lightweight, latency-critical text queries. Recent multimodal serving systems mitigate this interference through disaggregation, isolating encoding and inference stages across GPUs. While effective at large scale, such designs introduce severe GPU underutilization in more practical, moderate-scale deployments. In response, we build RPS-Serve a modality-aware scheduling framework that leverages the extreme heterogeneity in multimodal workloads and introduces a simple abstraction: videos behave like rocks, images like pebbles, and text like sand. Then, it lets sand flow quickly through pebbles and rocks, maximizing GPU utilization while ensuring fast responsiveness for lightweight requests and controlled execution for heavier ones. | **[Mind The Gap (MTG-AD): Dataset and Fine-grained Evaluation for End-to-End Inline Audio Descriptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39955v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Audio descriptions (AD) are essential for making visual media accessible to blind and low-vision (BLV) users. | **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. | **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Stateful Streaming Inference for Multimodal LLMs on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge SoCs combine CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, yet runtimes for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) keep generation on one engine and leave others idle. Streaming workloads allow ingestion and answering to overlap, but backend-local KV state binds queries to the engine that built their history. Stage performance and co-running interference vary, so no single placement wins across SoCs and workloads. \name separates logical history from engine-specific KV state so any language engine can continue a stream. Stateful Streaming Generation overlaps ingestion and answering, while Concurrent Sequence Generation serves ready queries in parallel. Phase-level state continuation lets one engine prefill a query and another decode the answer, freeing the first to resume ingestion. State-Driven Execution Planning (SDEP) chooses paths by predicted completion time and sets each engine's share of queries using arrival rates and measured service times. On M4 Max, SDEP cuts live-stream p95 time to first token by $76\%$, with every first token within one second, and raises throughput under backlog by up to $35\%$ over Metal-only execution with context reuse. On adapted VirtualHome and RoboCasa benchmarks, CausGround improves vision-based high-level planning across MLLM backbones, increasing average task success from 24.9% to 53.3% on VirtualHome and closed-loop planning success from 43.0% to 57.0% on RoboCasa. On SWITCH, average interaction reasoning accuracy increases from 25.8% to 31.9%, indicating improved action selection, recovery, and verification. Moreover, real-world experiments show that CausGround reasons backward from MLLM-interpreted goals to derive executable high-level action sequences for sequential manipulation. | **[Generalized CycleGRPO for Fine-Grained Multimodal Understanding and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29552v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained multimodal intelligence requires models to both understand a designated region and localize it from language. Existing methods learn these directions with separate objectives and task-specific annotations. We present Generalized CycleGRPO, the first unified reinforcement learning framework coupling region understanding and localization across spatial, temporal, and spatio-temporal domains. Given a region, a multimodal large language model generates candidate descriptions and then localizes each description to reconstruct the input region. Reconstruction overlap provides a verifiable reward for captioning, while individual localization rewards improve the inverse mapping. A common region-space formulation extends this cycle from image masks and boxes to video intervals and timestamped spatial evidence, without reference captions or an external language-model judge during reinforcement learning. In videos, the cycle encourages descriptions that distinguish a target event from similar recurrences. We introduce DOVE (Distinctive Occurrence-level Video-caption Evaluation), a human-verified contrastive benchmark for this temporal distinctiveness. CycleGRPO improves SAMTok's overall gIoU from 57.5% to 68.2% on GroundingSuite and average gIoU from 71.2% to 78.7% on GRES. For temporal tasks, it achieves 51.9% tIoU on Charades and 33.3% Temporal Evidence Coverage on DOVE, compared with 45.6% and 14.8% for the Qwen3-VL initialization. Models, code, and DOVE are available at https://devinxzhang.github.io/Generalized-CycleGRPO-Page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 14 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |

| **[LLMSeg: Training-Free Text-Prompted Image Segmentation with B-Splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29409v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Promptable segmentation models such as SAM 3 achieve remarkable open-vocabulary performance but require dedicated architectures, large-scale annotation engines, and task-specific training. | **[Small Average Error, Wide Individual Error: General-Purpose LLMs Estimating Energy Intake from Meal Photos in a Feeding Trial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26538v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Photo-based estimation of food energy could ease dietary assessment, and general-purpose multimodal large language models (LLMs) make such estimates easy to obtain. (2) Continuous Engagement evaluates sustained participation through continuous visual tracking and visually grounded query interaction. (3) Interaction Coordination evaluates how models coordinate listening and speaking under overlapping and multi-party interactions. Experiments show that leading real-time models achieve sub-second average response latency, yet their average window response rates remain at or below 53.77%. | **[LucidLyra: Feed-Forward Generative 3D Scene Reconstruction and Joint Understanding via Multi-Task Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40495v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present LucidLyra, a framework that makes generatively reconstructed 3D scenes directly queryable for semantics and instances in a feed-forward manner from video latents. While generative 3D reconstruction can synthesize virtual environments from a single image, downstream applications in robotics and physical AI require not only visual realism but also access to scene semantics and object identities. Existing 3D understanding approaches, however, require captured multi-view scenes, have limited generalization capabilities, or rely on expensive per-scene processing and optimization. To bridge this gap, our method distills complementary knowledge from a camera-controlled video diffusion model and frozen 2D vision foundation models into a unified model to jointly infer 3D Gaussians encoding geometry, appearance, open-vocabulary semantics, and class-agnostic instance cues. The resulting scenes support novel-view rendering, open-vocabulary segmentation, and object grouping without per-scene optimization, while training requires neither manual 3D annotations nor captured multi-view data. Our method outperforms baselines combining state-of-the-art 3DGS segmentation approaches with generative 3D scene reconstruction on curated synthetic and real-image-conditioned benchmarks, while preserving reconstruction quality. Furthermore, our latent formulation naturally extends to monocular video, reconstructing structured dynamic 3D scenes across time. By turning renderable but unstructured outputs into queryable 3D worlds, LucidLyra enables downstream applications including 3D scene editing, 3D asset mining, and robotic simulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/sil/projects/lucidlyra</p></details> |
| **[Understanding Temporal Semantic Stability in Open-Vocabulary UAV Perception through Metric 3D Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28665v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent open-vocabulary segmentation models have significantly advanced semantic perception for UAVs, for aerial and remote-sensing imagery, yet predictions from moving platforms can remain temporally inconsistent across repeated observations of the same physical scene. While semantic segmentation is typically evaluated on single images, temporal reliability remains less explored despite its importance for long-horizon robotic perception. In this work, we investigate temporal semantic stability by associating frame-wise predictions with persistent world-space locations through metric 3D fusion. We introduce a voxel-level evaluation framework that jointly characterises final semantic agreement, Semantic Belief Drift (SBD), Observation Persistence (OP), and semantic uncertainty from accumulated evidence. Experiments on UAVid-3D-Scenes reveal substantial frame-wise semantic variation and show that high aggregate world-space agreement can overstate temporal stability when many locations have limited repeated-observation support. Persistence-stratified analysis shows that recurrent voxels expose substantially greater semantic disagreement, while higher-persistence voxels exhibit lower direct consecutive semantic-evidence divergence. This behaviour is observed across two segmentation backbones and remains consistent under variations in voxel resolution, geometric association, and temporal sampling density. Overall, conditions that reduce world-space recurrence can increase apparent aggregate stability, demonstrating that semantic consistency must be interpreted together with observation support. Our findings establish observation persistence as an essential conditioning variable for evaluating long-horizon semantic reliability and provide a framework for analysing temporal semantic behaviour beyond image-level prediction.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 17th Indian Conference on Computer Vision, Graphics and Image Processing (ICVGIP 2026)</p></details> |

As a byproduct, the activations of DEX can also be decoded to distortion coefficients to support camera calibration. Code available at: https://github.com/Suchisrit/DEX.</p></details> |  |
| **[TileShare: Training-Free Acceleration of Overlapping Tiled ViT Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32233v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fixed-window Vision Transformer (ViT) decoders run at deployment resolution as overlapping tiles: every token in an overlap band is processed by several full decoder instances. In the 2.4B-parameter MiniMax-H3 video decoder, about half of all per-token computation is duplicated. Removing the tiles fails because attention, global tokens and coordinates are bound to them. TileShare keeps each released tile's attention and computes every other operation once per token. The released computation changes at one kind of step only: an overlap token's per-tile results are blended into one shared state with the released weights. Interventions show that this blend costs quality where attention reads the shared states, mainly in the first few layers, which TileShare therefore keeps per tile. Without training, keeping six layers per tile decodes 1.38-1.51x faster than batched and compiled tiling on two GPU types (up to 1.96x with FP8) and is equivalent to the released decoder in PSNR, LPIPS and DISTS on DAVIS videos. On unseen UVG videos with the setting frozen beforehand, it matches PSNR and DISTS and improves LPIPS. TileShare carries over to two further decoders and is again confirmed on unseen data. It is equivalent in PSNR and DISTS on MAGI-1. On the ViTok-v2 image autoencoder at 2K-4K, it matches overlapping tiling in PSNR, improves LPIPS and DISTS and decodes 1.5-1.8x faster. How many layers stay per tile is measured once per model (for decoders, without labels); we report it for 14 decoders and encoders. Optionally, two GPU-hours of self-distillation let MiniMax-H3 share every layer without a loss in PSNR, and a coordinate field trained while the network stays frozen brings fully shared CLIP-based open-vocabulary segmentation to parity with sliding windows on seven benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main paper + supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Query-Time Calibration of CLIP Features for Open-Vocabulary 3D Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30908v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lifting vision-language features such as CLIP into 3D Gaussian reconstructions enables open-vocabulary segmentation through natural-language queries. Our method partially subtracts a shared scene direction from visual features and text prototypes and whitens text prototypes using a fixed reference vocabulary of generic terms. It then uses soft clustering for multiclass prediction and CSLS with a relevance margin against the reference vocabulary for single-query prediction. Beyond reducing directional concentration, scene centering improves class-relative feature separation in every evaluated scene. Across seven lifting methods and four datasets, the resulting query-time calibration consistently improves mean intersection over union in both multiclass and single-query segmentation. | **[OpenEarth-OVS44: A Multimodal Remote Sensing Dataset for Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29642v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In remote sensing, open-vocabulary segmentation is particularly important for flexible and scalable land-cover understanding across diverse regions, as it improves generalization to novel categories without requiring exhaustive pixel-level annotations. Three evaluation tracks are provided: an RGB-only fine-grained benchmark, a multimodal track with diverse synthetic cloudy conditions for weather-robust evaluation, and a held-out cross-region track. Extensive experiments on representative open-vocabulary segmentation models demonstrate that training on OpenEarth-OVS44 substantially improves fine-grained segmentation performance over the strongest training-free baseline, with improvements of up to 7.1 mIoU points on unseen categories and 27.6 mIoU points on seen categories. | **[OV-Foam: How 3D Representations Shape Semantic Feature Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28131v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing feature lifting approaches for open-vocabulary segmentation rely on simple backprojection to assign features to primitives. Motivated by this insight, we propose OV-Foam, which uses PowerFoam as the underlying representation. This representation uses a power-diagram to partition the scene, resulting in zero overlap between primitives and a substantial reduction in primitives per ray, which ultimately simplifies the lifting problem and allows for closer-to-optimal feature assignment. We show that the space partitioning primitives of OV-Foam reach near-optimal assignment, and our experiments demonstrate improved open-vocabulary segmentation over Gaussian baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 10 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |

| **[GeoBridge: Evolving Transferable Decoder Topologies for Geometric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15280v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Depth estimation and Gaussian reconstruction share a need for multi-scale feature processing, yet use different prediction interfaces and training objectives. First, GeoBridge makes stage-internal topology searchable through residual, sequential, and parallel composition of configurable primitives. Second, a functional-tree encoding supports stage-aware crossover and hierarchical mutation, while a gradient sensitivity-alignment proxy evaluates candidates without training. Multi-objective evolution balances this proxy with computation and parameter cost. Third, selected topologies are instantiated and trained in target systems with task-specific prediction interfaces. A single source search takes approximately 12 GPU hours. GeoBridgeNet-A3 achieves the best depth accuracy among four adapted decoder baselines under a common encoder and training protocol on NYU Depth V2 and KITTI. The same topology reaches 27.11 dB PSNR in Gaussian novel-view synthesis on RealEstate10K, and its refined representations support open-vocabulary segmentation on LERF. | **[Training-Free Target-Aware Refinement of 3D Gaussian Language Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21619v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distilling vision-language features into 3D Gaussian Splatting yields a language map that can be queried with arbitrary text. Guided by a text-promptable segmenter, the visual embedding inside each detected target region is blended with the target text embedding, a convex combination whose coefficient sets the amount of injected prior, while non-target regions are left untouched. Injecting too much overwrites appearance cues and general queryability, so we seek, for each target, the smallest coefficient that still lets it win, and we obtain every coefficient in closed form, leaving no hyperparameters to tune. We further identify a failure mode of injection in language-goal localization. Project page: https://lab-of-ai-and-robotics.github.io/target-aware-langmap/</p></details> |

Experiments on multiple mammography datasets show that the proposed method consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation baselines on both base and novel lesion categories, while reducing the gap to fully supervised models that require dense target-domain masks. Lang3DSeg achieves 52.8% mIoU on nuScenes validation and 41.4% on SemanticKITTI, the highest among published annotation-free methods on both benchmarks. Our method leverages temporal coherence across consecutive frames by monitoring and combining complementary aspects of domain shift (visual change, adapter mismatch, and semantic drift) to trigger adaptation only when needed. We validate our approach on a benchmark including indoor and outdoor environments and using real robotic data. We demonstrate that our approach maintains segmentation accuracy while substantially reducing adaptations, making training-free adaptation practical and feasible for long-term, real-world robotic deployments.</p></details> |  |

EviSplat retains individual observation features within class-agnostic 3D instances that represent objects, object parts, or background regions. It also learns, for each Gaussian, a distribution describing which visual appearances its observations support. Given a text query, EviSplat scores each instance using its most relevant observations. The principal comparison isolates the ink set. Two patches share all seventeen recorded optimization settings and differ only in the colors available to them. One is constrained to a uniform color cube; the other to a gamut measured by printing and scanning a 216-patch chart. Each was optimized at three seeds and evaluated against thirteen victim conditions, with every rate reported against a size-matched optimized control. The effect of the measured gamut is victim-dependent rather than uniform. Net attack success rises on three of six closed-set segmentation victims, and for these the seed ranges of the two ink sets are disjoint: $+0.120$ on DeepLabv3-R101 and $+0.041$ on SegFormer-B0. The color-cube patch is consistently stronger on the open-vocabulary segmenter and on two of four detectors, though no detector exceeds a net of $+0.026$ under either ink set. The natural explanation is that a printable palette is simply less chromatic and lower in frequency than a digital one. Eleven further patches test this account and it does not hold. Once cardinality is matched, a palette as chromatic as the cube attacks equally well. Cardinality itself shows no trend from three inks to thirty-two. Palettes matched on cardinality, lightness and chroma, and differing only in hue placement, span $0.035$ to $0.136$. An instruction manager first tracks navigation progress and identifies the active landmark query. An instruction-conditioned perception mechanism then invokes open-vocabulary segmentation when the queried landmark is visible and its metric location can inform the next decision. | **[ProClosure: Hierarchical Room-Object Assignment using Progressive Boundary Closure from Monocular Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12614v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A 3D scene graph groups objects into rooms. A SLAM front end and an open-vocabulary segmenter supply a structural point cloud, camera trajectory and object tracks. The cloud is rasterised into a top-down map, rooms are recovered from it, and each object takes the room holding most of its extent. The difficulty lies in the map itself. Walls are recorded only where the camera looked, so a gap in the boundary may be a doorway or a stretch of wall that was never observed; nothing distinguishes the two. Prior methods treat both as passages, merging rooms that should remain separate. We observe that both require the same treatment: a room should not extend across either, so both are closed and need not be distinguished. Such an opening closes under a small amount of boundary growth, and few sightlines cross it, so points in different rooms rarely see one another. We use the first to recover rooms and the second to assign objects to them. Rooms are obtained by Progressively thickening the boundary inward and freezing each free-space region once it becomes enclosed, so every opening seals at its own scale rather than at a radius fixed in advance. | **[MAVISEG: Manifold Propagation and Visual Prototypes for Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Segmentation in Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05878v1)** | 2026-08-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-image diffusion transformers learn about objects and scenes by learning to generate them, making them strong candidates for training-free zero-shot open-vocabulary semantic segmentation.
