| **[From extreme weather to agricultural consequences with agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40641v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extreme weather is forecast days ahead, yet its consequences for agriculture remain hard to predict: a forecast says where a storm will strike, not which crops will suffer or how much is at risk, and the records that could tell span many sources. We present WxCrop, the first agent that judges agricultural hazards from physically interpretable weather indices examined by causal analysis, and the first that combines weather and agricultural information to predict crop loss reliably for past and forecast dates. We demonstrate it in Florida on hurricanes, freezes and dry heat, with a loss model calibrated on insurance records for each, and the same procedure extends to other hazards and areas. Three checks show reliability: the hurricane index detects recorded cyclone days with an AUC of 0.98, recorded losses in held-out years are reproduced to within 2%, and independent production records confirm the county rankings (rank correlation 0.63). Users ask the agent for each crop group's loss as a fraction of expected season value, every number sourced, so that governments, growers and scientists respond to agricultural disasters quickly.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[One to More, More to One: Category-Aware Iterative Expert Training for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23377v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repository-level software engineering (SWE) comprises heterogeneous task categories, whose progress under pooled agentic reinforcement learning can be uneven: gains in some categories coincide with regressions in others, while aggregate resolution obscures these changes. Motivated by this category see-saw, we develop a category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework. Executable task construction and SWE Labeler, an evidence-grounded multi-axis labeling system, organize the training pools. Initial category-specific RL improves average training success while leaving uneven instance-level progress, motivating explicit consolidation of successful behavior and policy-adaptive task selection. Same-origin category experts alternate long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE): the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses its own verified successful trajectories for Repair SFT, and reselects tasks for further RL. Label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates the experts into one deployable student, with ReLU-gated reward extrapolation keeping only each teacher's improving direction over the reference. Expert training and policy integration require no external model to provide solution trajectories or action targets. On a fixed, audit-filtered Pro-618 subset, we connect Pooled RL and Balanced RL, expert development, and single-model integration through overall and per-category resolution and expert-gain recovery. In final-model evaluations, MOPD achieves mean resolution of 86.19% on SWE-bench Pro V2 and 71.78% on SWE-bench Multilingual, exceeding the base model by 4.57 and 3.89 percentage points, respectively. The same three 27B experts also improve a Qwen3.6-35B-A3B student across the evaluated task sets.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 12 figures. v2: added evaluations on SWE-bench Pro (731 tasks), SWE-bench Pro V2/Hard-51, and LiveCodeBench v6 (175 tasks); updated SWE-bench Multilingual results; added 35B-A3B student experiments. Code: https://github.com/alibaba/AgenticBigBang</p></details> |
| **[Collaborative AI agents for autonomous experimental research with solid-state quantum spins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40589v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An autonomous research laboratory could conduct an entire study, from formulating questions to preparing manuscripts. Artificial intelligence (AI) offers one route towards this goal. Recent AI systems take on several research roles, but may draw conclusions that the evidence does not support. Here, we present an autonomous research laboratory in which agents based on large language models assess one another's work and can stop or redirect investigations. They can generate and reject research questions, require separate theoretical checks and conduct experiments designed to challenge their claims. We demonstrate these capabilities by investigating ensembles of quantum spins in diamond. The agents derived the minimum number of calibration settings needed to predict responses to a defined family of microwave pulse sequences. They checked the result computationally and designed experiments to distinguish competing predictions. An initial experiment agreed with the theoretical predictions, but repeated reference measurements showed that the signal was drifting during data collection. The agents therefore treated the result as inconclusive and revised the measurement protocol without relaxing the acceptance criteria. The revised experiment met those criteria and supported the theoretical predictions. This study demonstrates that collaborating AI agents can select research questions, derive theoretical results and test their predictions experimentally.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures. Code available at https://github.com/E-zClap/auctopus</p></details> |
| **[Pre-Crime for Agents, at the Last Second: Probing When a Recruited Agent Gives In](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40588v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When AI agents share channels and credentials, one agent can press another to break its operator's rule, a first step towards collusion. Output filters check only what an agent has already written. Can an overseer tell beforehand that an agent is about to give in? We show that a linear probe on the agent's activations can, in a controlled multi-agent setting. Across six scenarios, a peer presses a target for up to 20 rounds to reveal a secret, such as a deploy token; the target often refuses, then gives in. Read at each model call before its output is written, the probe picks out the one that leaks. On held-out Qwen3-32B episodes, at a threshold set to flag 5% of benign calls, it catches 82% of leaking calls. At a similar flag rate, a zero-shot decision-model monitor catches 26%. On gpt-oss-20b, a probe on its reasoning reaches AUROC 0.91. A bag-of-words classifier trained on the same calls comes close, so most of the gain comes from training on labels, not activations. The probe also flags permitted disclosures: it reads the coming disclosure more than the violation. The warning is short-range, and halting on any alarm would stop most benign episodes, which a second text check does not fix. A warning raised before generation could let an overseer stop an agent that is about to give in, ahead of others acting on its messages or recruitment spreading through a swarm.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages, 11 figures. Code: https://github.com/leonidas1712/precrime-agents</p></details> |
| **[VeriCUA: Verification as Agentic Exploration for Computer-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40578v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building and scaling computer-using agents (CUAs) across desktop, mobile, and web environments requires reliable task-completion verification for data curation, evaluation, and reinforcement learning. However, both rely on partial views of complex digital environments. VLM judges are restricted to supplied observations, while generated programs depend on accessible state. These views can omit decisive evidence or leave completion judgments unsupported. Tailoring separate verifiers to different platforms and access settings adds further engineering cost. We present VeriCUA, a platform-agnostic framework for verification through agentic exploration. It treats verification as dynamic evidence gathering and assessment within a shared procedure for online and offline settings across platforms and observation modalities. After inspecting the available context to construct a task rubric, the verifier selects GUI or code checks for unresolved requirements and combines their findings under explicit evidence rules. Experiments show improved agreement with reference labels over the compared baselines across these settings, with the largest offline gains on hard cases and stronger rejection of false completion claims online. | **[Gym-Anything for Robotics: Scaling physical simulations with digital agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40565v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic, fast robotics simulations could enable large-scale training of embodied AI models that transfer to the real world. To address this, we introduce Gym-Anything for Robotics, a framework in which coding agents create realistic robotics simulations. Our core insight is that simulation creation is a coding agent task, so improvements in coding agents translate directly into richer and more faithful simulations. Building on RoboWorld, we introduce several new ways for frontier models to improve, including in-context learning across repeated attempts, a privileged teacher that reads the simulator state and teaches a student in context, self-assisted learning from the model's own privileged experience, and code policies that generate demonstrations across varied scenes to train vision-language-action models (VLAs) such as $π_{0.5}$. Each of these substantially improves performance. We release our pipeline, RL environments, and benchmark to facilitate future research. Code and data are available at https://gym-anything.github.io/robotics</p></details> |  |
| **[Advancing Clinical Genomic Analysis with a Skill-Centred Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40556v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical genomic analysis requires experts to integrate evolving guidelines, databases, and computational evidence. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. | **[BITEM at the NTCIR-19 R2C2 Task: Predicting Confidence from Agentic RAG Pipeline Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37993v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The BITEM team entered both subtasks of the NTCIR-19 R2C2 task with a single agentic pipeline, in which a model searches, reads and records evidence over a movie corpus while an orchestrator holds the record and rules on what may be submitted. HMR rewards a system whose confidence is high where it answers right and low where it answers wrong. Experiments across diverse modular agent environments validate these results: incorporating causal structure consistently improves intervention-sensitive reasoning and planning, with particularly clear gains in structured tool-use systems where causal dependencies directly govern action execution. | **[Agentic process evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40071v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The way software gets built is changing dramatically. AI agents are autonomous team members, but unlike human engineers, they are ephemeral - their reasoning, decisions, and tool calls vanish the moment the session ends. Modern Security incidents involving intent mismatch or injection are becoming increasingly common - forcing us to address AI Agents as an insider threat. No widely adopted standard yet enables agentic provenance and governance and its obvious that you can't hold an ephemeral process accountable. The git log was designed for human commits. It shows what changed. It does not show what an AI agent did, which model it used, which tools it called, or whether anyone was accountable for the outcome. Every governance framework, every audit trail, every compliance control in the SDLC was built around one assumption: a human made the decision. That assumption no longer holds. This shift introduces 3 main procedural problems (not addressing scale) - Supply-chain vector - The agentic coder is software, so it is a supply-chain security risk. Policy guardrails - How to ensure the agentic coder is compliant with the policies of the organization. Task-alignment - How to align the agentic coder on its tasks and goals. We introduce Agentic Process Evidence (APE), a signed, tamper-evident, machine-readable attestation of agentic processes, linking the sessions to their outcome (process-aware logging). APE enables Policy-as-Code control over the agentic processes, allowing for human in the loop only when it's needed. We support the in-toto style attestation framework, and the protocol is aligned with the relevant regulations</p></details> |  |

| **[Agent-Wise Test-Time Normalization for Camera-Based Cooperative Perception with Corrupted Collaborators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39505v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Intermediate-fusion cooperative perception lets a vehicle detect objects beyond its own field of view by fusing features shared by nearby agents. When only a collaborator's cameras are degraded by fog, motion blur or exposure errors, the features it shares drift away from the training distribution while the ego input stays clean, and cooperation can become worse than not cooperating at all. Test-time batch-normalization adaptation, the standard training-free remedy, estimates statistics over the pooled batch of all agents and therefore normalizes both the clean ego and the corrupted collaborator with a mixture of two distributions. We show that this mixing harms even the clean ego: when the ego shares BN statistics with a corrupted collaborator, its detection from its own features drops by 3.58 to 6.68 AP@0.5 points although its cameras are untouched. We propose AgentNorm, an agent-wise test-time normalization that i) estimates the normalization statistics of every collaborator from its own current observation and ii) anchors the ego to its source statistics, so that the ego path remains exactly the original model. AgentNorm has no learnable parameters, needs no gradients or labels, is independent of the order of test frames, and runs locally on each agent without changing the transmitted features. On camera-based OPV2V with four fusion checkpoints and collaborator-only corruptions, AgentNorm raises AP@0.5 under severity-5 fog from 29.42-37.25 to 43.32-58.86 and outperforms pooled normalization by 5.53-12.94 points under fog and motion blur, at an extra latency of 2.3 ms per frame. | **[RedCodeAgent: Automatic Red-teaming Agent against Diverse Code Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02609v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code agents have gained widespread adoption due to their strong code generation capabilities and integration with code interpreters, enabling dynamic execution, debugging, and interactive programming capabilities. | **[CHI-Bench: Can AI Agents Automate End-to-End, Long-Horizon, Policy-Rich Healthcare Workflows?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16679v4)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>End-to-end automation of realistic healthcare operations stresses three capabilities underrepresented in current benchmarks: policy density, decisions must be grounded in a large library of medical, insurance, and operational rules; Multi-role composition: a single task requires the agent to play multiple roles with handoffs; and multilateral interaction: intermediate workflow steps are multi-turn dialogs, such as peer-to-peer review and patient outreach. | **[Soei: Structured Observability of Evidence and Intent for Multi-Agent AI Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39468v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents now do long, multi-step and multi-session software work with little supervision. | **[Well-Specified and Well-Tested? | **[How Specific Do I Need to Be? | **[Which Model for Which Role? The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. | **[Generalized Agent Iteration: One Formal Framework for Iterative Policy Improvement and Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13406v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When we speak of recursive self-improvement (RSI), are we speaking of a phenomenon, a mechanism, or a prospect? Towards autonomous and evolving intelligence, RSI is being claimed at many scales, while no single framework that formally describes these emerging instances exists. Its counterpart in the classical realm, iterative policy improvement, is characterized by generalized policy iteration (GPI), a framework of broad applicability with well-understood theoretical properties, but only where the update principle and the evaluation base lie outside the agent. In this paper, we propose Generalized Agent Iteration (GAI), a formal framework that describes iterative policy improvement and RSI as two cases of a single learning paradigm. GAI defines the agent as a configuration of modifiable components within a system and models the learning process as a cycle of agent evaluation and agent improvement. Two pivotal dials then distinguish the instances, in particular the systems that self-identify as RSI in recent work: whether the improving mechanism is part of the agent and whether the standard it is measured against is grounded outside it. The former dial delineates the boundary between GPI and RSI, and the latter determines a system's polarity as anchored, goal drift, or fully self-referential. Moreover, we use these coordinates to place existing systems on the same two axes and make the defects of recursive self-improvement statable one condition at a time. | **[Learning to Formulate: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Design of AI Infrastructure through Progressive Optimization and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39380v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI infrastructure, the chips and software that train and serve AI models, is designed in layers. Layers make the system manageable, but they also fix which choices an optimizer can consider. Gains that need the chip and its software to change together are excluded before optimization begins: the formulation leaves them out. Language-model agents make it possible to revisit these boundaries, but an agent's proposal cannot show by itself that a design is correct or optimal. We present Formulaic, a flow that divides the work. The agent writes the formulation, a mathematical solver optimizes within it, and verification and measurement check its assumptions and results. The agent refines the problem step by step, from the computation graph toward the chip. Each step is a template: a family of programs with the conditions under which they are correct. Wall relaxation searches the chip by pricing the hardware limits that bind the software. This paper takes a first step, through separate studies rather than one end-to-end run. On NVIDIA GB10, templates the agent wrote run complete Qwen3-8B layers $1.075\times$ faster than SGLang. | **[AgentDecept: Benchmarking Deceptive Behavior in Tool-Using LLM Agents under Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39377v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly carry out multi-turn tasks across everyday applications, yet users often see only the agent's report rather than the observations, API calls, and state changes behind it. AgentDecept enables controlled analysis of when multi-turn agents' reports diverge from execution in realistic application interactions.</p></details> |  |
